========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, um... well, I guess I'm here because things have just gotten really, really out of hand lately. I work down at the commercial laundry place, you know, the one that does all the hotel sheets and uniforms and stuff? It's just constant steam and industrial presses all day, and lately, I just... I keep making myself throw up after I eat. 

It's like, I'll eat something on my break, and then this panic sets in, just this huge overwhelming feeling, and I have to go do it. I've been doing it right there in the employee bathroom at work, which is so gross, honestly, with the bleach smell and the loud washing machines right outside the door. Or sometimes I have to hold it and I just rush through the front door right when I get home to do it. 

And it's just... I hate it. I really, really hate it. But it's like my brain just won't shut up until I do it. It has started feeling like it's literally the only way I can calm down, you know? Like the pressure just builds and builds with the heat at work and just... everything in my life, and making myself sick is the only release valve I have left. I just can't seem to stop doing it no matter how much I tell myself I won't today, and I just didn't know what else to do but come here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, god, yeah. That's... that's practically all I think about. It's constantly just running on a loop in my head, honestly. Like, we have these uniforms at the laundry place, right? These thick, stiff pants and these heavy aprons we have to wear to protect our clothes from the bleach and the heat. And I swear, if I'm getting dressed for my shift at like 5 AM and my waistband feels even just a tiny bit tight under my work apron... it's just this immediate, crushing panic. My heart just drops right into my stomach. 

I step on the scale in my bathroom a lot. Probably way too much, like right when I wake up, after I use the bathroom, sometimes right before I walk out the front door just to be sure... and if that number goes up even a little, even just a fraction of a pound, I completely lose it. I panic like I have ruined absolutely everything. I get this terrifying, overwhelming feeling that I'm going to be completely fat by the end of the day if I don't do something to fix it immediately. 

I'll just be standing there at work, feeding these huge, heavy damp sheets into the giant industrial ironer, and the steam is just blasting me in the face, and all I can focus on is how my stomach feels pressing against my pants. It's like I can literally feel myself getting bigger right there on the factory floor while I'm working. So yeah, when I go into that awful bathroom to... you know, make myself sick... it's because I'm just so terrified of gaining weight. It's like this massive fear that just swallows me whole, and getting rid of the food is the only way I can convince myself I won't get fat. It just takes over everything, my whole day is just ruined by it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know... I mean, I really don't know if it would ever be enough. It's like, I try to picture what that would even look like, being at an "ideal" weight, but the goalpost just keeps moving, you know? Like, back when this all started getting really bad, I had this number in my head. I thought, 'Okay, if I just get down to this weight, I'll be fine. I'll stop. I won't need to do this anymore.' And I got there. I actually hit that number on the scale one morning before my shift. I remember I was standing in my bathroom, the one with the cracked tile near the sink, and I saw the number, and for like... maybe two seconds, I felt this rush of relief. But then, almost immediately, this other voice in my head just sneered at me and said, 'Well, if you got here this easily, you can probably lose another five pounds. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' 

So it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. I'd still be standing there at the laundry, feeding those endless heavy sheets into the press, sweating through my shirt, and obsessing over whether the lunch I just ate was going to ruin everything. The fear doesn't go away just because the number on the scale goes down. If anything, it almost gets worse, because then you have this new, lower weight that you have to fiercely protect. You have further to fall, I guess. 

And the throwing up... it's become so tied to that fear, but also just to the feeling of being full. If I feel food in my stomach, even if I'm technically at a weight I used to dream about, that feeling of fullness just triggers this absolute panic. It feels heavy and wrong, like a mistake I have to undo right that second. So, honestly? No. I don't think hitting an ideal weight would stop the urge. I think my brain is just wired now to panic whenever I eat, and throwing up is the only way it knows how to hit the reset button, regardless of what the scale says. It's exhausting, it really is, just constantly chasing this finish line that doesn't actually exist.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I mean, it's not just the throwing up, though that's a huge part of it, obviously. But on the days after I feel like I've just completely messed up and eaten way too much... it's like I have to punish myself, or, I don't know, balance the scales somehow? So I'll wake up and I'll just refuse to eat. I'll go the whole entire day without a single bite of anything. I'll tell myself, 'You don't get to eat today until it's dark out, until it's really late.' And I'll be at the cleaners, right? Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. 

And it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.
=====================================

Probably way too much, like right when I wake up, after I use the bathroom, sometimes right before I walk out the front door just to be sure... and if that number goes up even a little, even just a fraction of a pound, I completely lose it. I panic like I have ruined absolutely everything. I get this terrifying, overwhelming feeling that I'm going to be completely fat by the end of the day if I don't do something to fix it immediately. \n\nI'll just be standing there at work, feeding these huge, heavy damp sheets into the giant industrial ironer, and the steam is just blasting me in the face, and all I can focus on is how my stomach feels pressing against my pants. It's like I can literally feel myself getting bigger right there on the factory floor while I'm working. So yeah, when I go into that awful bathroom to... you know, make myself sick... it's because I'm just so terrified of gaining weight. It's like this massive fear that just swallows me whole, and getting rid of the food is the only way I can convince myself I won't get fat. It just takes over everything, my whole day is just ruined by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up if you were at a weight that you consider ideal?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I really don't know if it would ever be enough. It's like, I try to picture what that would even look like, being at an \"ideal\" weight, but the goalpost just keeps moving, you know? Like, back when this all started getting really bad, I had this number in my head. I thought, 'Okay, if I just get down to this weight, I'll be fine. I'll stop. I won't need to do this anymore.' And I got there. I actually hit that number on the scale one morning before my shift. I remember I was standing in my bathroom, the one with the cracked tile near the sink, and I saw the number, and for like... maybe two seconds, I felt this rush of relief. But then, almost immediately, this other voice in my head just sneered at me and said, 'Well, if you got here this easily, you can probably lose another five pounds. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' \n\nSo it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. I'd still be standing there at the laundry, feeding those endless heavy sheets into the press, sweating through my shirt, and obsessing over whether the lunch I just ate was going to ruin everything. The fear doesn't go away just because the number on the scale goes down. If anything, it almost gets worse, because then you have this new, lower weight that you have to fiercely protect. You have further to fall, I guess. \n\nAnd the throwing up... it's become so tied to that fear, but also just to the feeling of being full. If I feel food in my stomach, even if I'm technically at a weight I used to dream about, that feeling of fullness just triggers this absolute panic. It feels heavy and wrong, like a mistake I have to undo right that second. So, honestly? No. I don't think hitting an ideal weight would stop the urge. I think my brain is just wired now to panic whenever I eat, and throwing up is the only way it knows how to hit the reset button, regardless of what the scale says. It's exhausting, it really is, just constantly chasing this finish line that doesn't actually exist.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I mean, it's not just the throwing up, though that's a huge part of it, obviously. But on the days after I feel like I've just completely messed up and eaten way too much... it's like I have to punish myself, or, I don't know, balance the scales somehow? So I'll wake up and I'll just refuse to eat. I'll go the whole entire day without a single bite of anything. I'll tell myself, 'You don't get to eat today until it's dark out, until it's really late.' And I'll be at the cleaners, right? Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. \n\nAnd it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. \n\nAnd it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a lot of weight since this problem started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly? No. And I mean... that is probably the most maddening part of this entire nightmare. You would think, right? You'd think with all the starving all day, and the pacing around the neighborhood in the dark, and the... the throwing up every time I feel like I messed up... you'd think I'd be just wasting away. But I'm not. I haven't lost much of anything at all. It just kind of... stays exactly the same, or it just bounces up and down by the same three or four pounds over and over again. It drives me absolutely out of my mind. 

Like, I'll be at the shop, pulling these heavy, soaking wet rugs out of the commercial extractors, just sweating right through my uniform and feeling completely dizzy because I haven't let myself eat a single thing since the day before, and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, surely I've lost weight this week. I have to have.' But then I go home, and I step on that same scale in my bathroom, and it's the exact same number staring back at me. Or maybe it's a half a pound less, which just instantly comes right back the second I drink a glass of ice water. 

It makes me feel like such a massive failure, you know? Like I'm putting my body through absolute hell, I'm exhausting myself, I'm making my whole life miserable, and for what? My work pants still fit exactly the same. That stiff waistband still digs into my stomach the exact same way when I sit down on a stool to fold the smaller garments. I just don't understand how my body holds onto it all. It's like my body is just fighting me every single step of the way, holding onto every single ounce just to spite me. So, no, I haven't lost a lot of weight. I'm just... stuck. I'm stuck in this awful, exhausting loop and I don't even have the weight loss to show for it.

I just can't seem to stop doing it no matter how much I tell myself I won't today, and I just didn't know what else to do but come here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or how you look when you feel the need to make yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yeah. That's... that's practically all I think about. It's constantly just running on a loop in my head, honestly. Like, we have these uniforms at the laundry place, right? These thick, stiff pants and these heavy aprons we have to wear to protect our clothes from the bleach and the heat. And I swear, if I'm getting dressed for my shift at like 5 AM and my waistband feels even just a tiny bit tight under my work apron... it's just this immediate, crushing panic. My heart just drops right into my stomach. \n\nI step on the scale in my bathroom a lot. Probably way too much, like right when I wake up, after I use the bathroom, sometimes right before I walk out the front door just to be sure... and if that number goes up even a little, even just a fraction of a pound, I completely lose it. I get this terrifying, overwhelming feeling that I'm going to be completely fat by the end of the day if I don't do something to fix it immediately. \n\nI'll just be standing there at work, feeding these huge, heavy damp sheets into the giant industrial ironer, and the steam is just blasting me in the face, and all I can focus on is how my stomach feels pressing against my pants. It's like I can literally feel myself getting bigger right there on the factory floor while I'm working. I mean, I really don't know if it would ever be enough. It's like, I try to picture what that would even look like, being at an \"ideal\" weight, but the goalpost just keeps moving, you know? I remember I was standing in my bathroom, the one with the cracked tile near the sink, and I saw the number, and for like... maybe two seconds, I felt this rush of relief. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' \n\nSo it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' \n\nSo it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. I'd still be standing there at the laundry, feeding those endless heavy sheets into the press, sweating through my shirt, and obsessing over whether the lunch I just ate was going to ruin everything. The fear doesn't go away just because the number on the scale goes down. If anything, it almost gets worse, because then you have this new, lower weight that you have to fiercely protect. You have further to fall, I guess. \n\nAnd the throwing up... it's become so tied to that fear, but also just to the feeling of being full. If I feel food in my stomach, even if I'm technically at a weight I used to dream about, that feeling of fullness just triggers this absolute panic. It feels heavy and wrong, like a mistake I have to undo right that second. So, honestly? No. I don't think hitting an ideal weight would stop the urge. I think my brain is just wired now to panic whenever I eat, and throwing up is the only way it knows how to hit the reset button, regardless of what the scale says. It's exhausting, it really is, just constantly chasing this finish line that doesn't actually exist.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I mean, it's not just the throwing up, though that's a huge part of it, obviously. But on the days after I feel like I've just completely messed up and eaten way too much... it's like I have to punish myself, or, I don't know, balance the scales somehow? So I'll wake up and I'll just refuse to eat. I'll go the whole entire day without a single bite of anything. I'll tell myself, 'You don't get to eat today until it's dark out, until it's really late.' And I'll be at the cleaners, right? Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. \n\nAnd it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a lot of weight since this problem started?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. And I mean... that is probably the most maddening part of this entire nightmare. You would think, right? You'd think with all the starving all day, and the pacing around the neighborhood in the dark, and the... the throwing up every time I feel like I messed up... you'd think I'd be just wasting away. But I'm not. I haven't lost much of anything at all. It just kind of... stays exactly the same, or it just bounces up and down by the same three or four pounds over and over again. It drives me absolutely out of my mind. \n\nLike, I'll be at the shop, pulling these heavy, soaking wet rugs out of the commercial extractors, just sweating right through my uniform and feeling completely dizzy because I haven't let myself eat a single thing since the day before, and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, surely I've lost weight this week. I have to have.' But then I go home, and I step on that same scale in my bathroom, and it's the exact same number staring back at me. Or maybe it's a half a pound less, which just instantly comes right back the second I drink a glass of ice water. \n\nIt makes me feel like such a massive failure, you know? Like I'm putting my body through absolute hell, I'm exhausting myself, I'm making my whole life miserable, and for what? My work pants still fit exactly the same. That stiff waistband still digs into my stomach the exact same way when I sit down on a stool to fold the smaller garments. I just don't understand how my body holds onto it all. It's like my body is just fighting me every single step of the way, holding onto every single ounce just to spite me. So, no, I haven't lost a lot of weight. I'm just... stuck. I'm stuck in this awful, exhausting loop and I don't even have the weight loss to show for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle since this problem started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My period? No... no, actually, they're exactly the same. Which, honestly, is just another thing that completely baffles me about all of this. You hear about women who stop getting their periods when they, you know, when they put their bodies through too much stress or when they mess with their food too much. And I am definitely putting my body through a ridiculous amount of stress. I mean, between the throwing up, and the fasting, and then just the sheer physical labor at the cleaners... I'm lifting these massive, soaking wet bundles of hotel linens, dealing with the intense heat from the steam presses all day long. My body is just constantly running on empty, right? So you'd think my cycle would just give up. You'd think my body would say, 'Okay, we can't handle this right now, we need to shut down.'

But nope. Every single month, right on schedule, like absolute clockwork. It's almost insulting, honestly, like my body is just mocking me. I'll be in the middle of a ten-hour shift, already feeling completely lightheaded and my legs are aching from standing on that concrete, and then on top of it all, the regular cramps start. And I have to run to the awful little employee bathroom in the back—the one that always smells like bleach and mildew—and just deal with it. It's just as heavy, it lasts the exact same five or six days it always has since I was a teenager. I dropped out of high school back then to start working, so I've been on my feet dealing with this kind of exhaustion for a really long time, but my cycle has just never skipped a beat. 

So, yeah, no changes at all. It just keeps coming every single month, adding to the whole miserable routine, even though I feel like I'm literally falling apart everywhere else. It really makes no sense to me.

But it's like my brain just won't shut up until I do it. It has started feeling like it's literally the only way I can calm down, you know? Like the pressure just builds and builds with the heat at work and just... everything in my life, and making myself sick is the only release valve I have left. I just can't seem to stop doing it no matter how much I tell myself I won't today, and I just didn't know what else to do but come here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or how you look when you feel the need to make yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yeah. That's... that's practically all I think about. It's constantly just running on a loop in my head, honestly. Like, we have these uniforms at the laundry place, right? These thick, stiff pants and these heavy aprons we have to wear to protect our clothes from the bleach and the heat. And I swear, if I'm getting dressed for my shift at like 5 AM and my waistband feels even just a tiny bit tight under my work apron... it's just this immediate, crushing panic. My heart just drops right into my stomach. \n\nI step on the scale in my bathroom a lot. Probably way too much, like right when I wake up, after I use the bathroom, sometimes right before I walk out the front door just to be sure... and if that number goes up even a little, even just a fraction of a pound, I completely lose it. I panic like I have ruined absolutely everything. I get this terrifying, overwhelming feeling that I'm going to be completely fat by the end of the day if I don't do something to fix it immediately. \n\nI'll just be standing there at work, feeding these huge, heavy damp sheets into the giant industrial ironer, and the steam is just blasting me in the face, and all I can focus on is how my stomach feels pressing against my pants. It's like I can literally feel myself getting bigger right there on the factory floor while I'm working. So yeah, when I go into that awful bathroom to... you know, make myself sick... it's because I'm just so terrified of gaining weight. It's like this massive fear that just swallows me whole, and getting rid of the food is the only way I can convince myself I won't get fat. It just takes over everything, my whole day is just ruined by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up if you were at a weight that you consider ideal?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I really don't know if it would ever be enough. It's like, I try to picture what that would even look like, being at an \"ideal\" weight, but the goalpost just keeps moving, you know? Like, back when this all started getting really bad, I had this number in my head. I thought, 'Okay, if I just get down to this weight, I'll be fine. I'll stop. I won't need to do this anymore.' And I got there. I actually hit that number on the scale one morning before my shift. I remember I was standing in my bathroom, the one with the cracked tile near the sink, and I saw the number, and for like... maybe two seconds, I felt this rush of relief. But then, almost immediately, this other voice in my head just sneered at me and said, 'Well, if you got here this easily, you can probably lose another five pounds. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' \n\nSo it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. I'd still be standing there at the laundry, feeding those endless heavy sheets into the press, sweating through my shirt, and obsessing over whether the lunch I just ate was going to ruin everything. The fear doesn't go away just because the number on the scale goes down. If anything, it almost gets worse, because then you have this new, lower weight that you have to fiercely protect. You have further to fall, I guess. \n\nAnd the throwing up... it's become so tied to that fear, but also just to the feeling of being full. If I feel food in my stomach, even if I'm technically at a weight I used to dream about, that feeling of fullness just triggers this absolute panic. It feels heavy and wrong, like a mistake I have to undo right that second. So, honestly? No. I don't think hitting an ideal weight would stop the urge. I think my brain is just wired now to panic whenever I eat, and throwing up is the only way it knows how to hit the reset button, regardless of what the scale says. It's exhausting, it really is, just constantly chasing this finish line that doesn't actually exist.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I mean, it's not just the throwing up, though that's a huge part of it, obviously. But on the days after I feel like I've just completely messed up and eaten way too much... it's like I have to punish myself, or, I don't know, balance the scales somehow? So I'll wake up and I'll just refuse to eat. I'll go the whole entire day without a single bite of anything. I'll tell myself, 'You don't get to eat today until it's dark out, until it's really late.' And I'll be at the cleaners, right? Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. \n\nAnd it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a lot of weight since this problem started?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. And I mean... that is probably the most maddening part of this entire nightmare. You would think, right? You'd think with all the starving all day, and the pacing around the neighborhood in the dark, and the... the throwing up every time I feel like I messed up... you'd think I'd be just wasting away. But I'm not. I haven't lost much of anything at all. It just kind of... stays exactly the same, or it just bounces up and down by the same three or four pounds over and over again. It drives me absolutely out of my mind. \n\nLike, I'll be at the shop, pulling these heavy, soaking wet rugs out of the commercial extractors, just sweating right through my uniform and feeling completely dizzy because I haven't let myself eat a single thing since the day before, and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, surely I've lost weight this week. I have to have.' But then I go home, and I step on that same scale in my bathroom, and it's the exact same number staring back at me. Or maybe it's a half a pound less, which just instantly comes right back the second I drink a glass of ice water. \n\nIt makes me feel like such a massive failure, you know? Like I'm putting my body through absolute hell, I'm exhausting myself, I'm making my whole life miserable, and for what? My work pants still fit exactly the same. That stiff waistband still digs into my stomach the exact same way when I sit down on a stool to fold the smaller garments. I just don't understand how my body holds onto it all. It's like my body is just fighting me every single step of the way, holding onto every single ounce just to spite me. So, no, I haven't lost a lot of weight. I'm just... stuck. I'm stuck in this awful, exhausting loop and I don't even have the weight loss to show for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle since this problem started?\n\nPatient: My period? No... no, actually, they're exactly the same. Which, honestly, is just another thing that completely baffles me about all of this. You hear about women who stop getting their periods when they, you know, when they put their bodies through too much stress or when they mess with their food too much. And I am definitely putting my body through a ridiculous amount of stress. I mean, between the throwing up, and the fasting, and then just the sheer physical labor at the cleaners... I'm lifting these massive, soaking wet bundles of hotel linens, dealing with the intense heat from the steam presses all day long. My body is just constantly running on empty, right? So you'd think my cycle would just give up. You'd think my body would say, 'Okay, we can't handle this right now, we need to shut down.'\n\nBut nope. Every single month, right on schedule, like absolute clockwork. It's almost insulting, honestly, like my body is just mocking me. I'll be in the middle of a ten-hour shift, already feeling completely lightheaded and my legs are aching from standing on that concrete, and then on top of it all, the regular cramps start. And I have to run to the awful little employee bathroom in the back—the one that always smells like bleach and mildew—and just deal with it. It's just as heavy, it lasts the exact same five or six days it always has since I was a teenager. I dropped out of high school back then to start working, so I've been on my feet dealing with this kind of exhaustion for a really long time, but my cycle has just never skipped a beat. \n\nSo, yeah, no changes at all. It just keeps coming every single month, adding to the whole miserable routine, even though I feel like I'm literally falling apart everywhere else. It really makes no sense to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, God, yes. I mean, people will tell me I look fine, or my sister will say I'm crazy for worrying about it, but... they just don't see what I see. It's like we're looking at two completely different people. They look at me and see whatever it is they see, but I have to actually live in this body, you know? 

It happens all the time at work. I can be having a really, really good day. Like, I'm keeping up with the steam presses, I'm getting all the tough stains out of the collars, the boss is happy because we're actually ahead of schedule on the big hotel orders for once... I can be doing good at the shop, really feeling like I'm on top of things and actually competent at my job. I'll be practically running back and forth, feeling strong, feeling like I'm actually worth something. 

But then... it's the front windows. We have these huge, floor-to-ceiling plate glass windows at the front of the cleaners, right behind where the motorized conveyor racks are. And if I have to walk up to the front to hang up a finished order in the plastic wrap, I'll just happen to catch my reflection in the glass. 

And it just... it instantly ruins everything. Because all I can see is how huge I look. I'll catch that reflection in the glass and my stomach or my hips will just look so big to me. Like, massive. I'll see the way my work pants are sitting on my hips, or how my stomach just seems to push out against my shirt, and it's literally all I can focus on. It's like everything else in the room just fades away and all I can see is this giant, disgusting person in the window. 

And instantly, all that pride I had about doing a good job just completely vanishes. I just feel like a total failure. It doesn't matter how many heavy canvas carts of wet towels I managed to haul around that morning, or how hard I worked. If I look in that glass and see those hips staring back at me, my whole day is just destroyed right then and there. I'll just want to crawl into the back supply closet and hide behind the detergent drums so no one has to look at me. So, yeah, it really doesn't matter what anyone else says to me. They can tell me I look fine until they're blue in the face, but they're not the ones who have to look in that glass and see what I see.

It just takes over everything, my whole day is just ruined by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up if you were at a weight that you consider ideal?\n\nPatient: I don't know... Like, back when this all started getting really bad, I had this number in my head. I thought, 'Okay, if I just get down to this weight, I'll be fine. I'll stop. I won't need to do this anymore.' And I got there. I actually hit that number on the scale one morning before my shift. I remember I was standing in my bathroom, the one with the cracked tile near the sink, and I saw the number, and for like... maybe two seconds, I felt this rush of relief. But then, almost immediately, this other voice in my head just sneered at me and said, 'Well, if you got here this easily, you can probably lose another five pounds. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' \n\nSo it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. I'd still be standing there at the laundry, feeding those endless heavy sheets into the press, sweating through my shirt, and obsessing over whether the lunch I just ate was going to ruin everything. The fear doesn't go away just because the number on the scale goes down. If anything, it almost gets worse, because then you have this new, lower weight that you have to fiercely protect. You have further to fall, I guess. \n\nAnd the throwing up... it's become so tied to that fear, but also just to the feeling of being full. If I feel food in my stomach, even if I'm technically at a weight I used to dream about, that feeling of fullness just triggers this absolute panic. It feels heavy and wrong, like a mistake I have to undo right that second. So, honestly? No. I don't think hitting an ideal weight would stop the urge. I think my brain is just wired now to panic whenever I eat, and throwing up is the only way it knows how to hit the reset button, regardless of what the scale says. It's exhausting, it really is, just constantly chasing this finish line that doesn't actually exist.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I mean, it's not just the throwing up, though that's a huge part of it, obviously. But on the days after I feel like I've just completely messed up and eaten way too much... it's like I have to punish myself, or, I don't know, balance the scales somehow? So I'll wake up and I'll just refuse to eat. I'll go the whole entire day without a single bite of anything. I'll tell myself, 'You don't get to eat today until it's dark out, until it's really late.' And I'll be at the cleaners, right? Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. \n\nAnd it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a lot of weight since this problem started?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. And I mean... that is probably the most maddening part of this entire nightmare. You would think, right? You'd think with all the starving all day, and the pacing around the neighborhood in the dark, and the... the throwing up every time I feel like I messed up... you'd think I'd be just wasting away. But I'm not. I haven't lost much of anything at all. It just kind of... stays exactly the same, or it just bounces up and down by the same three or four pounds over and over again. It drives me absolutely out of my mind. \n\nLike, I'll be at the shop, pulling these heavy, soaking wet rugs out of the commercial extractors, just sweating right through my uniform and feeling completely dizzy because I haven't let myself eat a single thing since the day before, and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, surely I've lost weight this week. I have to have.' But then I go home, and I step on that same scale in my bathroom, and it's the exact same number staring back at me. Or maybe it's a half a pound less, which just instantly comes right back the second I drink a glass of ice water. \n\nIt makes me feel like such a massive failure, you know? Like I'm putting my body through absolute hell, I'm exhausting myself, I'm making my whole life miserable, and for what? My work pants still fit exactly the same. That stiff waistband still digs into my stomach the exact same way when I sit down on a stool to fold the smaller garments. I just don't understand how my body holds onto it all. It's like my body is just fighting me every single step of the way, holding onto every single ounce just to spite me. So, no, I haven't lost a lot of weight. I'm just... stuck. manifestation: This has been going on for about six months, since I started trying to remake my habits and image for the sustainable-textile showcase.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost a lot of weight since this problem started?\n\nPatient: It drives me absolutely out of my mind. \n\nLike, I'll be at the shop, pulling these heavy, soaking wet rugs out of the commercial extractors, just sweating right through my uniform and feeling completely dizzy because I haven't let myself eat a single thing since the day before, and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, surely I've lost weight this week. I have to have.' But then I go home, and I step on that same scale in my bathroom, and it's the exact same number staring back at me. Or maybe it's a half a pound less, which just instantly comes right back the second I drink a glass of ice water. \n\nIt makes me feel like such a massive failure, you know? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... I guess? Intellectually, maybe. Like, I hear what you're asking, and I know my sister and my coworkers probably aren't just outright lying to me when they say I look normal. But it's... it's just so hard to actually believe it when the feeling I get is so incredibly overpowering. 

Like I was just saying about the shop, right? I can be doing really, really good at the shop. I can be flying through the orders, pressing fifty shirts an hour, feeling super capable and strong—like I actually matter and I'm doing something right for once. But then... I catch my reflection in that front glass, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me in that split second, none of that hard work even matters anymore. I still just instantly feel like an absolute failure. 

And I guess... I guess maybe that proves your point, right? Because logically, a normal person wouldn't let a reflection completely erase the fact that they just did a great job at work. But for me, it just wipes out everything good. It doesn't matter if a customer or my boss is standing right there telling me I look fine or I'm doing a great job. All I know is what I'm seeing in that glass, and what I'm seeing makes me feel so huge and so disgusted with myself that I can't even think straight. I just start obsessing over the way my uniform is clinging to me and how I need to fix it, how I need to fix myself. 

So, yeah, maybe my eyes are distorting things or my perception is completely off compared to the rest of the world. But even if I try to tell myself that, even if I try to rationalize it, the feeling of being this massive failure is just so real. It just completely swallows me whole, and I can't just turn it off just because someone else tells me they see something different.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, um... well, I guess I'm here because things have just gotten really, really out of hand lately. I work down at the commercial laundry place, you know, the one that does all the hotel sheets and uniforms and stuff? It's just constant steam and industrial presses all day, and lately, I just... I keep making myself throw up after I eat. \n\nIt's like, I'll eat something on my break, and then this panic sets in, just this huge overwhelming feeling, and I have to go do it. I've been doing it right there in the employee bathroom at work, which is so gross, honestly, with the bleach smell and the loud washing machines right outside the door. Or sometimes I have to hold it and I just rush through the front door right when I get home to do it. \n\nAnd it's just... I hate it. I really, really hate it. But it's like my brain just won't shut up until I do it. It has started feeling like it's literally the only way I can calm down, you know? Like the pressure just builds and builds with the heat at work and just... everything in my life, and making myself sick is the only release valve I have left. I just can't seem to stop doing it no matter how much I tell myself I won't today, and I just didn't know what else to do but come here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or how you look when you feel the need to make yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yeah. That's... that's practically all I think about. It's constantly just running on a loop in my head, honestly. Like, we have these uniforms at the laundry place, right? These thick, stiff pants and these heavy aprons we have to wear to protect our clothes from the bleach and the heat. And I swear, if I'm getting dressed for my shift at like 5 AM and my waistband feels even just a tiny bit tight under my work apron... it's just this immediate, crushing panic. My heart just drops right into my stomach. \n\nI step on the scale in my bathroom a lot. Probably way too much, like right when I wake up, after I use the bathroom, sometimes right before I walk out the front door just to be sure... and if that number goes up even a little, even just a fraction of a pound, I completely lose it. I panic like I have ruined absolutely everything. I get this terrifying, overwhelming feeling that I'm going to be completely fat by the end of the day if I don't do something to fix it immediately. \n\nI'll just be standing there at work, feeding these huge, heavy damp sheets into the giant industrial ironer, and the steam is just blasting me in the face, and all I can focus on is how my stomach feels pressing against my pants. It's like I can literally feel myself getting bigger right there on the factory floor while I'm working. So yeah, when I go into that awful bathroom to... you know, make myself sick... it's because I'm just so terrified of gaining weight. It's like this massive fear that just swallows me whole, and getting rid of the food is the only way I can convince myself I won't get fat. It just takes over everything, my whole day is just ruined by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up if you were at a weight that you consider ideal?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I mean, I really don't know if it would ever be enough. It's like, I try to picture what that would even look like, being at an \"ideal\" weight, but the goalpost just keeps moving, you know? Like, back when this all started getting really bad, I had this number in my head. I thought, 'Okay, if I just get down to this weight, I'll be fine. I'll stop. I won't need to do this anymore.' And I got there. I actually hit that number on the scale one morning before my shift. I remember I was standing in my bathroom, the one with the cracked tile near the sink, and I saw the number, and for like... maybe two seconds, I felt this rush of relief. But then, almost immediately, this other voice in my head just sneered at me and said, 'Well, if you got here this easily, you can probably lose another five pounds. And if you don't, you're just going to gain it all back.' \n\nSo it never actually feels like I'm safe. Even if I were somehow at this perfect weight, whatever that even means anymore, I think I'd still be terrified of losing it. I'd still be standing there at the laundry, feeding those endless heavy sheets into the press, sweating through my shirt, and obsessing over whether the lunch I just ate was going to ruin everything. The fear doesn't go away just because the number on the scale goes down. If anything, it almost gets worse, because then you have this new, lower weight that you have to fiercely protect. You have further to fall, I guess. \n\nAnd the throwing up... it's become so tied to that fear, but also just to the feeling of being full. If I feel food in my stomach, even if I'm technically at a weight I used to dream about, that feeling of fullness just triggers this absolute panic. It feels heavy and wrong, like a mistake I have to undo right that second. So, honestly? No. I don't think hitting an ideal weight would stop the urge. I think my brain is just wired now to panic whenever I eat, and throwing up is the only way it knows how to hit the reset button, regardless of what the scale says. It's exhausting, it really is, just constantly chasing this finish line that doesn't actually exist.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I mean, it's not just the throwing up, though that's a huge part of it, obviously. But on the days after I feel like I've just completely messed up and eaten way too much... it's like I have to punish myself, or, I don't know, balance the scales somehow? So I'll wake up and I'll just refuse to eat. I'll go the whole entire day without a single bite of anything. I'll tell myself, 'You don't get to eat today until it's dark out, until it's really late.' And I'll be at the cleaners, right? Just sweating and working those heavy presses, and my stomach will be absolutely screaming at me, just growling so loud you can almost hear it over the noise of the industrial washers, but I just chug ice water and push through it because I know I have to make up for whatever I did the day before. \n\nAnd it's not just holding off on the food, either. I also... I make myself move. A lot. Like, way more than I should. Standing at the cleaners for an eight or ten-hour shift on that hard concrete floor, it already just wrecks your legs. My calves cramp up, my feet throb in my work shoes, my lower back aches from hauling those huge, heavy canvas carts of wet hotel towels around all day. But if I ate too much, I can't just go home and sit down on the couch. I can't let myself rest. I'll force myself to go out and walk, or I'll do these really intense exercises in my living room. I'll just be pacing around my neighborhood for hours, walking block after block in the dark, even when my legs are literally shaking and shooting with pain from standing at work all day long. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I stop moving, the weight is going to permanently stick to me, so I just keep going until I can barely stand up anymore. I just have to burn it off, you know? It's so exhausting, my body just aches all the time, but I just can't let myself off the hook.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a lot of weight since this problem started?\n\nPatient: Honestly? But I'm not. I haven't lost much of anything at all. It just kind of... stays exactly the same, or it just bounces up and down by the same three or four pounds over and over again. It drives me absolutely out of my mind. \n\nLike, I'll be at the shop, pulling these heavy, soaking wet rugs out of the commercial extractors, just sweating right through my uniform and feeling completely dizzy because I haven't let myself eat a single thing since the day before, and I'll think to myself, 'Okay, surely I've lost weight this week. I have to have.' But then I go home, and I step on that same scale in my bathroom, and it's the exact same number staring back at me. Or maybe it's a half a pound less, which just instantly comes right back the second I drink a glass of ice water. \n\nIt makes me feel like such a massive failure, you know? Like I'm putting my body through absolute hell, I'm exhausting myself, I'm making my whole life miserable, and for what? My work pants still fit exactly the same. That stiff waistband still digs into my stomach the exact same way when I sit down on a stool to fold the smaller garments. I just don't understand how my body holds onto it all. It's like my body is just fighting me every single step of the way, holding onto every single ounce just to spite me. So, no, I haven't lost a lot of weight. I'm just... stuck. No... no, actually, they're exactly the same. Which, honestly, is just another thing that completely baffles me about all of this. You hear about women who stop getting their periods when they, you know, when they put their bodies through too much stress or when they mess with their food too much. And I am definitely putting my body through a ridiculous amount of stress. I mean, between the throwing up, and the fasting, and then just the sheer physical labor at the cleaners... I'm lifting these massive, soaking wet bundles of hotel linens, dealing with the intense heat from the steam presses all day long. My body is just constantly running on empty, right? So you'd think my cycle would just give up. You'd think my body would say, 'Okay, we can't handle this right now, we need to shut down.'\n\nBut nope. Every single month, right on schedule, like absolute clockwork. It's almost insulting, honestly, like my body is just mocking me. I'll be in the middle of a ten-hour shift, already feeling completely lightheaded and my legs are aching from standing on that concrete, and then on top of it all, the regular cramps start. And I have to run to the awful little employee bathroom in the back—the one that always smells like bleach and mildew—and just deal with it. It's just as heavy, it lasts the exact same five or six days it always has since I was a teenager. I dropped out of high school back then to start working, so I've been on my feet dealing with this kind of exhaustion for a really long time, but my cycle has just never skipped a beat. \n\nSo, yeah, no changes at all. It just keeps coming every single month, adding to the whole miserable routine, even though I feel like I'm literally falling apart everywhere else. It really makes no sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight even when others tell you that you don't?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. I mean, people will tell me I look fine, or my sister will say I'm crazy for worrying about it, but... they just don't see what I see. It's like we're looking at two completely different people. They look at me and see whatever it is they see, but I have to actually live in this body, you know? \n\nIt happens all the time at work. I can be having a really, really good day. Like, I'm keeping up with the steam presses, I'm getting all the tough stains out of the collars, the boss is happy because we're actually ahead of schedule on the big hotel orders for once... I can be doing good at the shop, really feeling like I'm on top of things and actually competent at my job. I'll be practically running back and forth, feeling strong, feeling like I'm actually worth something. \n\nBut then... it's the front windows. We have these huge, floor-to-ceiling plate glass windows at the front of the cleaners, right behind where the motorized conveyor racks are. And if I have to walk up to the front to hang up a finished order in the plastic wrap, I'll just happen to catch my reflection in the glass. \n\nAnd it just... it instantly ruins everything. Because all I can see is how huge I look. I'll catch that reflection in the glass and my stomach or my hips will just look so big to me. Like, massive. I'll see the way my work pants are sitting on my hips, or how my stomach just seems to push out against my shirt, and it's literally all I can focus on. It's like everything else in the room just fades away and all I can see is this giant, disgusting person in the window. \n\nAnd instantly, all that pride I had about doing a good job just completely vanishes. I just feel like a total failure. It doesn't matter how many heavy canvas carts of wet towels I managed to haul around that morning, or how hard I worked. If I look in that glass and see those hips staring back at me, my whole day is just destroyed right then and there. I'll just want to crawl into the back supply closet and hide behind the detergent drums so no one has to look at me. So, yeah, it really doesn't matter what anyone else says to me. They can tell me I look fine until they're blue in the face, but they're not the ones who have to look in that glass and see what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your own weight might be different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess? Intellectually, maybe. Like, I hear what you're asking, and I know my sister and my coworkers probably aren't just outright lying to me when they say I look normal. But it's... it's just so hard to actually believe it when the feeling I get is so incredibly overpowering. \n\nLike I was just saying about the shop, right? I can be doing really, really good at the shop. I can be flying through the orders, pressing fifty shirts an hour, feeling super capable and strong—like I actually matter and I'm doing something right for once. But then... I catch my reflection in that front glass, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me in that split second, none of that hard work even matters anymore. I still just instantly feel like an absolute failure. \n\nAnd I guess... I guess maybe that proves your point, right? Because logically, a normal person wouldn't let a reflection completely erase the fact that they just did a great job at work. But for me, it just wipes out everything good. It doesn't matter if a customer or my boss is standing right there telling me I look fine or I'm doing a great job. All I know is what I'm seeing in that glass, and what I'm seeing makes me feel so huge and so disgusted with myself that I can't even think straight. I just start obsessing over the way my uniform is clinging to me and how I need to fix it, how I need to fix myself. \n\nSo, yeah, maybe my eyes are distorting things or my perception is completely off compared to the rest of the world. But even if I try to tell myself that, even if I try to rationalize it, the feeling of being this massive failure is just so real. It just completely swallows me whole, and I can't just turn it off just because someone else tells me they see something different.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know I literally just said this, like, two seconds ago, but... yeah. Yes. It absolutely is. It's honestly the only thing that matters to me, which sounds so pathetic out loud, but it's the truth. 

Like I keep saying about work... and I know I'm rambling about the cleaners a lot today, but it just perfectly explains how my brain works. I try so hard to build my self-worth on other things, you know? Since I don't have a diploma or anything fancy, I just have my work ethic. So I try to tell myself, 'You have value because you work hard.' And I can be doing so good at the shop, right? Like, really pulling my weight, getting through all those heavy canvas carts of wet towels, sweating over the presses, being the most reliable person there. For a minute, I might actually feel proud of myself. 

But it's just a house of cards. Because the second I walk up to the front and catch my reflection in that big glass window... if my stomach or my hips look big to me, which they almost always do, it's just over. All that pride, all that supposed self-worth from being a good employee? It just evaporates. I still just feel like an absolute failure. 

It just overrides everything else I am or everything else I do. It really doesn't matter if my boss is thrilled with me, it doesn't matter if I'm a good sister, it doesn't matter how many hours I stand on my feet and push through the exhaustion. If I look in that glass and I don't like the shape of my body, I am a failure. That's just the bottom line for me. It dictates my entire mood, my entire day, my entire... well, my entire life, really. It's like my brain just can't process any other kind of success if my body isn't perfectly the way I want it. So, yeah... I'd say it definitely determines my self-worth. Pretty much entirely.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... it definitely causes problems. Especially with my sister, and even the girls I work with at the cleaners. Because... well, my whole life, my entire day basically just gets arranged around hiding what happened. Like, if I eat something, or if I mess up, I can't just let it go and hang out with people. I just can't. 

So, like, the girls at the shop, they're always wanting to grab a bite after our shift. You know, we'll finish up, turn off the presses, close out the registers, and they'll want to go to the diner down the street just to unwind. But I... I end up skipping those after-work meals all the time. I always have some excuse ready. My sister gets so frustrated with me because I'll just bail on her, too. She'll say, 'Come on, you just worked a ten-hour shift on your feet, just come sit with us.' But I can't do it. I can't sit there and be normal with them because I am just so completely preoccupied with how I'm going to make up for eating earlier in the day. 

It's honestly exhausting. It's like my brain just locks onto it and I can't focus on anything else. I'll even find myself rushing through my actual work tasks—like, practically throwing the plastic covers over the dry cleaning or hurrying through the sorting bins—just so I can get out of there. And then I'm rushing through my errands on the way home, too. Like, I'll be practically running through the grocery store or the pharmacy, not even paying attention to what I'm doing, because all I can think about is getting home so I can deal with it in private. 

I have to hide it, you know? I can't let them see how crazy I am about it, or what I have to do to fix it. So I just push everyone away. I skip the dinners, I rush through my life, and I just isolate myself so I can manage this... this whole secret routine I have. So, yeah, it definitely hurts my relationships. People just think I'm flaky or that I don't want to spend time with them, but they don't realize that my entire schedule is just built around hiding this.
